ATT&CKT1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Registry
// Article-specific bespoke detection — Snyk VulnBench JS 1.0: Can LLMs Find the Same Bugs Twice?
// Hunts the actual binaries / paths / commandline fragments named
// in the article instead of a generic technique-class template.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName in~ ("server.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName,
          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

// File-creation events for the named binaries / paths
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (FolderPath has_any ("/var/app/public/") or FileName in~ ("server.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

``` Article-specific bespoke detection — Snyk VulnBench JS 1.0: Can LLMs Find the Same Bugs Twice? ```
| tstats `summariesonly` count earliest(_time) AS firstTime latest(_time) AS lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process_name IN ("server.js"))
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name,
       Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_path
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)`
| append [
| tstats `summariesonly` count
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("created","modified")
      AND (Filesystem.file_path="*/var/app/public/*" OR Filesystem.file_name IN ("server.js"))
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.process_name,
       Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
]

    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.process_name,
       Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name

CISA KEV: CVE-2026-48558 — SimpleHelp Authentication Bypass Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-06-29 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

SimpleHelp contains an authentication bypass vulnerability in the OIDC authentication flow. When OIDC authentication is configured, identity tokens submitted during login are accepted without verifying their cryptographic signature. In a vulnerable configuration, a remote, unauthenticated attacker can submit a forged token containing arbitrary identity claims to obtain a fully authenticated technician session. In some configurations, this may also allow bypass of multi-factor authentication. Vendor: SimpleHelp , Product: SimpleHelp. Federal patch due: 2026-07-02.

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-48558")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-48558")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

[GHSA / CRITICAL] CVE-2026-53519: Nezha Monitoring: Pre-auth path traversal via /dashboard.. prefix confusion leaks jwt_secret_key

Nezha Monitoring: Pre-auth path traversal via /dashboard.. prefix confusion leaks jwt_secret_key

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Nezha Monitoring CVE-2026-53519 pre-auth path traversal via /dashboard.. prefix confusion
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.

Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Nezha CVE-2026-53519 confirmed secret disclosure — 200 response to /dashboard.. traversal · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
Nezha Monitoring CVE-2026-53519 pre-auth path traversal via /dashboard.. prefix confusion AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound HTTP requests whose path uses the 'dashboard..' / 'dashboard%2e%2e' / 'dashboard..%2f' prefix-confusion token that escapes the admin-dist root in Nezha's NoRoute fallback handler. This is the exact unauthenticated file-read primitive of CVE-2026-53519 (fixed in 2.0.13) used to reach data/config.yaml and data/sqlite.db. Rationale: The token 'dashboard' immediately followed by '..'/'%2e%2e'/'..%2f' (no separating slash) is the exact bypass string from the advisory PoC and has no benign form — legitimate asset requests are always '/dashboard/'. Cross-checked NVD (CVSS 9.1, fixed 2.0.13) and public PoC. ImWebSession chosen for ASIM portability; W3CIISLog (csUriStem+csUriQuery) or CommonSecurityLog (RequestURL) work identically if the tenant proxies via IIS/WAF instead. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53519 • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-5c25-7vpj-9mqh • https://github.com/tar-xz/CVE-2026-53519-PoC
ATT&CKT1190 T1083
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized vulnerability scanners (Nuclei/Tenable/Qualys) firing the CVE-2026-53519 template against internal Nezha instances
  • Internal red-team or pentest validating the patch on 2.0.13
  • Security researcher replaying the PoC in a lab that ships logs to the same SIEM
  • A WAF that URL-decodes and re-logs the request, producing a duplicate 'dashboard..' event per hit
Suppression filter
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("10.0.0.0") // add authorized scanner/pentest source IPs here to suppress sanctioned CVE-2026-53519 template runs
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: pull the full request path and HTTP status for the source IP; a 200 with Content-Type application/yaml or a sqlite body means the secret was served (pivot to UC2). Check the Nezha version banner — anything <2.0.13 is vulnerable. 2) CONTAIN: block the source IP at the WAF/edge, take the Nezha dashboard off the public internet, and immediately rotate jwt_secret_key AND agent_secret_key in data/config.yaml (invalidates any forged admin JWT), then force-logout all sessions. 3) HUNT: search the same source IP for follow-on '/dashboard../data/sqlite.db' reads and for anomalous admin logins/API-token use after the request time; upgrade to 2.0.13+.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Url contains "/dashboard.." or Url contains "/dashboard%2e" or Url contains "dashboard..%2f" or Url contains "dashboard%2e%2e"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DvcAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 8f2c1d64-9b3e-4a77-bf01-2a6d5e9c7f10
description: Detects unauthenticated path traversal against Nezha Monitoring exploiting the strings.HasPrefix /dashboard prefix-confusion bug (CVE-2026-53519) to read data/config.yaml or data/sqlite.db.

Nezha CVE-2026-53519 confirmed secret disclosure — 200 response to /dashboard.. traversal AIActions on Objectives High
Alerts when a Nezha prefix-confusion traversal request returns HTTP 200, meaning http.ServeFile actually served the escaped file (jwt_secret_key in data/config.yaml or the full data/sqlite.db). Distinct from UC1: this fires only on successful exfil, not blocked/404 attempts, so it is a confirmed-breach signal warranting immediate secret rotation. Rationale: Combines the CVE-2026-53519 bypass token with sc-status 200 and the two default-deployment target files named in the advisory (data/config.yaml holding the HS256 jwt_secret_key, data/sqlite.db holding admin bcrypt hashes) — a 200 means http.ServeFile returned the file, so this is confirmed disclosure not a probe. Corroborated by NVD and the public PoC repo. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53519 • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-5c25-7vpj-9mqh • https://github.com/tar-xz/CVE-2026-53519-PoC
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized scanner whose template asserts on the 200 + config.yaml body (Nuclei matcher) against an internal Nezha
  • Pentest confirming exploitability before the 2.0.13 upgrade
  • A caching reverse proxy replaying a cached 200 for the same crafted URL to a second analyst-driven request
  • WAF in detect-only mode logging both the request and an upstream 200 it did not block
Suppression filter
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("10.0.0.0") // allowlist sanctioned scanner/pentest IPs; keep all external-origin hits in scope
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: treat as confirmed breach — the response body (bytes_out/HttpResponseBodyBytes near the config size, ~167 bytes for config.yaml) shows the secret left the server; identify which file (config.yaml vs sqlite.db) from the URL. 2) CONTAIN: immediately rotate jwt_secret_key and agent_secret_key, invalidate all dashboard sessions, and rotate any OAuth2 client secrets / GitHub release token / GeoIP key also stored in config.yaml; if sqlite.db was served, force-reset all dashboard user passwords and revoke API tokens. 3) HUNT: correlate the source IP and the disclosure time against subsequent admin sign-ins, new API-token creation, and server-registry changes in Nezha; take the instance offline until upgraded to 2.0.13+.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpStatusCode == 200
| where Url contains "/dashboard.." or Url contains "/dashboard%2e" or Url contains "dashboard..%2f" or Url contains "dashboard%2e%2e"
| where Url has_any ("config.yaml", "sqlite.db", ".yaml", ".db")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, Url, HttpStatusCode, HttpResponseBodyBytes, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Nezha CVE-2026-53519 Successful Secret Disclosure (200 on /dashboard.. traversal)
id: c41a7e29-5d8b-4f16-9e2a-7b03d6a11e58
description: Detects a successful (HTTP 200) unauthenticated file read via the Nezha /dashboard.. prefix-confusion traversal, indicating data/config.yaml (jwt_secret_key) or data/sqlite.db was served.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-5c25-7vpj-9mqh
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53519

  category: webserver
detection:
  selection:
    cs-uri-stem|contains:
      - '/dashboard..'
      - '/dashboard%2e%2e'
      - 'dashboard..%2f'
      - 'dashboard%2e%2e'
    sc-status: 200
  target:
    cs-uri-stem|contains:
      - 'config.yaml'
      - 'sqlite.db'
  condition: selection and target
level: critical

| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Web.bytes_out) as bytes_out from datamodel=Web where Web.status=200 AND (Web.url="*dashboard..*" OR Web.url="*dashboard%2e%2e*" OR Web.url="*dashboard..%2f*") AND (Web.url="*config.yaml*" OR Web.url="*sqlite.db*" OR Web.url="*.yaml*" OR Web.url="*.db*") by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.url, Web.http_user_agent | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime

Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs Actions on Objectives High
Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies SQLite from Chrome/Edge/Firefox.
ATT&CKT1539 T1555.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\Google\Chrome\User Data\", @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\", @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\")
| where FileName in~ ("Login Data","Cookies","logins.json","cookies.sqlite")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath, FileName, ActionType

// ASIM ImFileEvent — covers MDE, Sysmon, AuditD, Defender for Cloud, etc.
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where TargetFilePath has_any (@"\Google\Chrome\User Data\",
                                  @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\",
                                  @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\")
| where TargetFileName in~ ("Login Data","Cookies","logins.json","cookies.sqlite")
| where ActingProcessName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, EventType

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where (Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`

Nezha WebSocket terminal/file-manager session hijack — one stream UUID attached from 2+ source IPs AIExploitation High
Hunts the GHSA-q6xx-5vr8-p898 cross-tenant RCE: the same live /ws/terminal/<uuid> or /ws/file/<uuid> stream UUID is attached to from two or more distinct client IPs. Legitimately exactly one client (the creator) connects per stream UUID, so a second concurrent attach on a live UUID is the hijack itself. Rationale: Uses the article's exact vulnerable endpoints /ws/terminal/:id and /ws/file/:id and the invariant that createTerminal returns one UUID to one client, so >1 distinct attaching source IP on a single UUID is high-fidelity for the hijack. Defender/Datadog/Falcon/CloudWatch left empty because the discriminating field (the UUID in the WebSocket URL path) lives only in reverse-proxy HTTP logs, which EDR/endpoint/cloud-audit telemetry does not carry. Cross-checked against: • https://dailycve.com/nezha-monitoring-websocket-session-hijacking-cross-tenant-rce-ghsa-q6xx-5vr8-p898-critical-dc-jun2026-727/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/github.com/nezhahq/nezha/CVE-2026-47124/ • https://github.com/nezhahq/nezha
ATT&CKT1190 T1563
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Single user roaming mid-session (laptop→phone, VPN/Wi-Fi flip) so one live UUID legitimately sees two source IPs.
  • Dashboard client opening dual-stack connections logged as separate IPv4 and IPv6 client addresses for the same session.
  • Reverse proxy behind Cloudflare/LB not forwarding X-Forwarded-For — real client IP collapses to edge IP (causes false negatives) or NAT makes two users share one IP.
  • Link-preview / error-reporter bots (Slack unfurl, Sentry) fetching the WebSocket URL harvested from a Referer/breadcrumb — benign but non-owner attach.
  • Uptime/synthetic monitor replaying a captured session URL.
Suppression filter
| extend DistinctSubnets = array_length(set_difference(Clients, dynamic([]))) | extend NetSet = set_difference(zip(Clients), dynamic([])) | where DistinctClients > 1 and not (Endpoint == "" ) // narrow to cross-subnet: replace with dcount over strcat(split(SrcIpAddr,'.')[0..2]) computed in summarize to drop same-/24 roaming and dual-stack pairs
Response runbook
1) Triage: Pull every request for the flagged StreamId, order by time; the first source IP is the creator, later IP(s) are suspect. Correlate each source IP's dashboard session (proxy auth cookie / SigninLogs) to a user and confirm the second IP is a different principal, not the owner's dual-stack/VPN address. 2) Contain: If a distinct principal, terminate the live session (kill the ws / restart dashboard), treat the target server as compromised — rotate its SSH keys and credentials (terminal = full shell) — disable the suspected RoleMember, and upgrade Nezha to ≥ v2.0.10. 3) Hunt: Sweep that source IP for other /ws/terminal//ws/file/ UUIDs it touched, inspect the target host for post-exploit artifacts (files written via /ws/file/, new cron, reverse shells), and audit who can read the reverse-proxy logs / Sentry breadcrumbs where UUIDs leak.
let lookback = 3d;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where Url has "/ws/terminal/" or Url has "/ws/file/"
| extend StreamId = extract(@"/ws/(?:terminal|file)/([0-9a-fA-F\-]{16,})", 1, Url)
| where isnotempty(StreamId)
| extend Endpoint = extract(@"/ws/(terminal|file)/", 1, Url)
| summarize DistinctClients = dcount(SrcIpAddr), Clients = make_set(SrcIpAddr, 20), Users = make_set(SrcUsername, 20), Hits = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by StreamId, Endpoint
| where DistinctClients > 1   // exactly one legitimate client attaches per stream UUID; 2+ distinct source IPs on one live UUID = cross-tenant hijack
| order by LastSeen desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rex field=url "/ws/(?<endpoint>terminal|file)/(?<stream_id>[0-9a-fA-F\-]{16,})"
| where isnotnull(stream_id) AND distinct_clients>1
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
| sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false dc(Web.src) as distinct_clients values(Web.src) as clients values(Web.user) as users count as hits min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Web.Web where (Web.url="*/ws/terminal/*" OR Web.url="*/ws/file/*") by Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rex field=url "/ws/(?<endpoint>terminal|file)/(?<stream_id>[0-9a-fA-F\-]{16,})"

| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
| sort - lastTime
Nezha harvested stream-UUID replay — one client attaching to many distinct terminal/file WebSocket UUIDs AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the leaked-UUID abuse path in GHSA-q6xx-5vr8-p898: an attacker who scraped stream UUIDs from proxy logs / Referer / Sentry breadcrumbs replays them, causing one source IP (or account) to attach to an abnormally high number of distinct /ws/terminal/ or /ws/file/ UUIDs in a short window. Rationale: Targets a different pivot than UC1 (fan-out by one client across many UUIDs vs. one UUID across many clients), matching the article's note that attackers passively harvest UUIDs from side channels then replay them. Threshold of >5 distinct UUIDs/hour separates a UUID sweep from a normal operator opening a single session. Non-web telemetry left empty for the same reason as UC1 — the UUID only exists in the reverse-proxy URL path. Cross-checked against: • https://dailycve.com/nezha-monitoring-websocket-session-hijacking-cross-tenant-rce-ghsa-q6xx-5vr8-p898-critical-dc-jun2026-727/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/github.com/nezhahq/nezha/CVE-2026-47120/
ATT&CKT1078 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate RoleAdmin/operator managing many servers who opens terminals/file managers across the fleet in one session.
  • Automated provisioning or bulk-maintenance tooling that opens file-manager sessions to many hosts from one jump box IP.
  • Shared NAT/VPN egress collapsing several distinct operators to a single source IP, inflating per-IP UUID counts.
  • A single long troubleshooting session that reconnects repeatedly, generating multiple UUIDs from one client.
  • Load balancer or WAF re-issuing requests so the same logical session appears as several UUID hits.
Suppression filter
| where SrcUsername !in~ ("") and DistinctStreams > 5 and SrcUsername !has "admin"  // optional: exclude known fleet-admin service accounts once their normal per-hour session baseline is established
Response runbook
1) Triage: Confirm whether the high-fan-out SrcIpAddr/SrcUsername maps to a known admin/operator with legitimate multi-server duties; if unknown or a RoleMember, treat as suspicious. Check whether the UUIDs it hit were created by other users (cross-tenant) vs. its own. 2) Contain: If cross-tenant replay is confirmed, disable the account, block the source IP at the proxy, terminate active sessions, and upgrade Nezha to ≥ v2.0.10 to enforce IsStreamAuthorizedForUser. 3) Hunt: Pivot each hijacked UUID into UC1 to find the true owner and target servers, review those hosts for shell/file-write abuse, and identify the leak channel (proxy log access, Sentry/Bugsnag breadcrumbs, Referer) that exposed the UUIDs.
let lookback = 1d;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where Url has "/ws/terminal/" or Url has "/ws/file/"
| extend StreamId = extract(@"/ws/(?:terminal|file)/([0-9a-fA-F\-]{16,})", 1, Url)
| where isnotempty(StreamId)
| summarize DistinctStreams = dcount(StreamId), Streams = make_set(StreamId, 50), Hits = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| where DistinctStreams > 5   // one client hitting >5 distinct terminal/file stream UUIDs in an hour indicates replay of harvested UUIDs, not normal single-session use
| order by DistinctStreams desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`

    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:

        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'

            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'

            - '\opera.exe'

mcp-pinot MCP server accepting non-loopback inbound connections on 0.0.0.0:8080 AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-49257 exposure condition: a Python/uvicorn process running mcp-pinot accepting inbound TCP connections on port 8080 from a non-loopback, public source. Pre-v3.1.0 the server binds 0.0.0.0:8080 with OAuth off, so any inbound connection from off-host is a candidate for unauthenticated tool invocation against the Pinot cluster. Rationale: Anchors on the article's exact exposure primitive — the mcp-pinot Python/uvicorn process (default bind 0.0.0.0:8080) accepting inbound from a non-loopback/public source, the precondition the v3.1.0 fix removes by rebinding to 127.0.0.1. Command-line anchor 'mcp_pinot' tightens it; port 8080 alone is noisy, hence hunting tier. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-73cv-556c-w3g6 • https://www.akamai.com/blog/security-research/one-fluke-3-pattern-mcp-back-end-vulnerabilities • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/cve-2026-49257 • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-49257-cwe-306-missing-authentication-for--c0c28b77341e3a12
ATT&CKT1190 T1133
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate internal MCP client (LLM agent host) on another subnet reaching the server for authorized Pinot queries
  • DevOps/monitoring health-check probes hitting :8080 from a scanner or load balancer
  • Kubernetes readiness/liveness probes or service-mesh sidecar connecting to the pod on 8080
  • Another unrelated Python web service (Flask/uvicorn) legitimately bound to 8080 on the host
  • Internal vulnerability scanner (Nessus/Qualys) sweeping port 8080
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("healthz","readiness","liveness")) and RemoteIP !in ("<known-agent-host-ip>")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the listening process is mcp-pinot and its version (<3.1.0 is vulnerable); check whether OAUTH_ENABLED and MCP_HOST are set. Verify the source IP is expected (known agent host vs. arbitrary/off-net). Step 2 (contain): If exposure is confirmed and unintended, rebind to 127.0.0.1 (MCP_HOST=127.0.0.1) or set OAUTH_ENABLED=true and restart; upgrade to v3.1.0; add a host/network ACL restricting 8080. Rotate the PINOT_TOKEN / PINOT_USERNAME service credentials the server holds. Step 3 (hunt): Pull web/proxy logs for POST /mcp from that source (see companion UC), and review Pinot controller logs for unexpected schema/table-config creation (e.g. rogue schemas) and heavy SELECTs during the exposure window.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where LocalPort == 8080
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("python","python3","python.exe","uv","uv.exe","uvicorn")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("mcp_pinot","mcp-pinot")
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
| where RemoteIP !in ("127.0.0.1","::1")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, LocalIP, LocalPort, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstPortNumber == 8080
| where NetworkDirection == "Inbound"
| where isnotempty(SrcIpAddr)
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1","::1")
| where not(ipv4_is_private(SrcIpAddr))
| summarize FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), Attempts = count() by SrcIpAddr, DstIpAddr, DstHostname, DstPortNumber, NetworkDirection
| order by LastSeen desc

| ContextImageFileName=/(python3?|uv|uvicorn)/i
| NOT cidr("127.0.0.0/8", field=RemoteAddressIP4)
| NOT cidr("10.0.0.0/8", field=RemoteAddressIP4)
| NOT cidr("172.16.0.0/12", field=RemoteAddressIP4)
| NOT cidr("192.168.0.0/16", field=RemoteAddressIP4)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, LocalPort, RemoteAddressIP4], function=count())

Unauthenticated JSON-RPC tool invocation on mcp-pinot /mcp endpoint (no Authorization header) AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the CVE-2026-49257 confused-deputy invocation: an HTTP POST to the /mcp JSON-RPC endpoint that carries no Authorization header, from a non-loopback source, returning success. In the default (OAuth-off) config these calls execute read_query/create_schema/update_table_config against Pinot with the server's privileged credentials. Rationale: Uses the article's exact endpoint path (/mcp), the JSON-RPC POST verb from the reproduction curl, and the defining unauth condition — an empty Authorization header (ImWebSession.HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization). Combining path + missing auth + success + non-loopback source is high-fidelity because a correctly configured server would either require OAuth (auth header present) or bind loopback-only (no external source). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-73cv-556c-w3g6 • https://www.akamai.com/blog/security-research/one-fluke-3-pattern-mcp-back-end-vulnerabilities • https://dailycve.com/mcp-pinot-unauthenticated-remote-code-execution-via-default-configuration-cve-2026-49257-critical-dc-jun2026-701/
ATT&CKT1190 T1213 T1565.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized MCP agent host legitimately calling /mcp while OAuth is intentionally disabled on an isolated network
  • Web proxy that strips/does not log the Authorization header, making authenticated calls look unauthenticated
  • Health-check or uptime monitor issuing POST /mcp with an empty JSON-RPC body
  • Security scanner probing the /mcp path
  • Internal API gateway that terminates OAuth upstream and forwards to /mcp without a per-request bearer token
Suppression filter
| where UserAgent !in~ ("kube-probe","ELB-HealthChecker/2.0") and SrcIpAddr !in ("<known-agent-host-ip>")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the target host runs mcp-pinot <3.1.0 and whether OAuth is expected; inspect the source IP and user-agent — automated tooling vs. curl/python-requests is a strong signal. Check response codes for a burst of successful 200s. Step 2 (contain): Block the source IP at the proxy/WAF, force OAUTH_ENABLED=true or MCP_HOST=127.0.0.1, restart, and upgrade to v3.1.0; rotate the Pinot service account credentials the server proxies with. Step 3 (hunt): Correlate to Pinot controller logs for create_schema/update_table_config/reload_table_filters activity and unusually expensive read_query SELECTs originating in the same window; enumerate any new/altered Pinot schemas or table configs and validate against change tickets.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod == "POST"
| where Url has "/mcp"
| where isempty(HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization)
| where HttpStatusCode in (200, 202)
| where isnotempty(SrcIpAddr) and SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1","::1")
| where not(ipv4_is_private(SrcIpAddr))
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstIpAddr, DstHostname, DstPortNumber, Url, HttpMethod, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Malicious litellm_init.pth startup hook dropped in site-packages (CVE-2026-42208) AIInstallation High
Hunts creation of the credential-stealer `litellm_init.pth` startup hook shipped by the compromised litellm 1.82.7/1.82.8 wheels, which Python auto-executes on interpreter startup with no import. Article-specific because the filename and site-packages location are unique to this supply-chain payload. Rationale: The `litellm_init.pth` filename in `site-packages/` is a unique, high-fidelity artifact of the CVE-2026-42208 payload named directly in the advisory and confirmed by Snyk/NetSPI/BerriAI issue #24512 (34,628-byte .pth). No legitimate package ships a file with this exact name. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/BerriAI/litellm/issues/24512 • https://snyk.io/blog/poisoned-security-scanner-backdooring-litellm/ • https://www.netspi.com/blog/executive-blog/ai-ml-pentesting/litellm-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-42208
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1546.016
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A local security-audit or IR script writing a decoy litellm_init.pth as a canary/test file
  • Backup or file-integrity tooling restoring an archived site-packages tree containing the artifact
  • Developer intentionally reproducing the IOC in a sandbox for research
  • A remediation script quarantining the file to a new path (rename event)
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("pip.exe","pip3.exe","python.exe","python3.exe","pip","uv.exe") or ActionType == "FileCreated"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the SHA256 of the dropped file and the InitiatingProcess (expect pip/python during install); identify which project's requirements pulled litellm 1.82.7/1.82.8. 2) Contain: isolate the host, delete the litellm_init.pth, and force-rotate every credential reachable from that environment (AWS/GCP/Azure keys, SSH keys, kubeconfig, DB creds, CI/CD secrets). 3) Hunt: search all build agents and dev workstations for the same filename, pin litellm>=1.83.7,!=1.82.8, and pivot to the network beacon UC (models.litellm.cloud) to confirm exfil occurred.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "litellm_init.pth"
| where FolderPath has "site-packages"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "litellm_init.pth"
| where TargetFilePath has "site-packages"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

litellm supply-chain exfil beacon to models.litellm.cloud (CVE-2026-42208) AICommand & Control High
Hunts outbound connections/DNS to models.litellm.cloud, the exfiltration endpoint the compromised litellm wheel POSTs AES-256/RSA-encrypted stolen credentials to. Article-specific because this domain is the hard-coded C2/exfil sink for CVE-2026-42208, not a generic egress heuristic. Rationale: models.litellm.cloud is the exact hard-coded exfil endpoint cited in the advisory and independently confirmed by Snyk, NetSPI, Miggo and BerriAI issue #24512 as the AES-256/RSA POST sink; any resolution of it is a confirmed second-source IOC, not a heuristic. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/BerriAI/litellm/issues/24512 • https://snyk.io/blog/poisoned-security-scanner-backdooring-litellm/ • https://www.miggo.io/post/rce-in-litellm-cve-2026-42208-how-two-vulnerabilities-and-36-hours-turn-an-ai-gateway-into-a-backdoor • https://www.netspi.com/blog/executive-blog/ai-ml-pentesting/litellm-supply-chain-compromise/
ATT&CKT1567 T1041 T1552.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A threat-intel/EDR product performing sinkhole or reputation lookups against the known-bad domain
  • A SOC analyst manually browsing to the domain during triage
  • A DNS security appliance pre-resolving blocklist entries for enrichment
  • Passive DNS/telemetry-replay tooling replaying historic captures
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the InitiatingProcess making the request (expect python.exe/pip) and whether the connection succeeded vs was blocked; correlate the host with the litellm_init.pth file UC. 2) Contain: block models.litellm.cloud and *.litellm.cloud at proxy/firewall, isolate the host, and treat all locally reachable secrets as compromised — rotate immediately. 3) Hunt: sweep DNS/proxy logs org-wide for any prior resolution of the domain to scope every affected build agent and developer host, and audit CI/CD secret stores for anomalous use of the exposed credentials.

// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push

                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),

        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)

        | where SHA256 in~ ("71e35aef03099cd1f2d6446734273025a163597de93912df321ef118bf135238", "a0d229be8efcb2f9135e2ad55ba275b76ddcfeb55fa4370e0a522a5bdee0120b", "6cf223aea68b0e8031ff68251e30b6017a0513fe152e235c26f248ba1e15c92a") or SHA1 in~ ("71e35aef03099cd1f2d6446734273025a163597de93912df321ef118bf135238", "a0d229be8efcb2f9135e2ad55ba275b76ddcfeb55fa4370e0a522a5bdee0120b", "6cf223aea68b0e8031ff68251e30b6017a0513fe152e235c26f248ba1e15c92a") or MD5 in~ ("71e35aef03099cd1f2d6446734273025a163597de93912df321ef118bf135238", "a0d229be8efcb2f9135e2ad55ba275b76ddcfeb55fa4370e0a522a5bdee0120b", "6cf223aea68b0e8031ff68251e30b6017a0513fe152e235c26f248ba1e15c92a")

| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["71e35aef03099cd1f2d6446734273025a163597de93912df321ef118bf135238", "a0d229be8efcb2f9135e2ad55ba275b76ddcfeb55fa4370e0a522a5bdee0120b", "6cf223aea68b0e8031ff68251e30b6017a0513fe152e235c26f248ba1e15c92a"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Backpropagate training UI publicly exposed via 'backprop ui --share' (CVE-2026-48797) AIDelivery High
Hunts process executions of the Backpropagate CLI launching the Reflex training UI with the --share (public exposure) or --auth flags. Pre-1.2.0 the --auth control is inert, so any --share launch stands up an unauthenticated training control plane; --share --auth is the false-safety case operators believed was protected. Rationale: Uses the exact CLI invocation surface from the advisory (backprop ui + --share/--auth). --share is the article's documented public-exposure flag and pre-1.2.0 makes the UI reachable with no working auth; matching --auth alongside surfaces the false-safety deployments the advisory calls out. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f65r-h4g3-3h9h • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/backpropagate/CVE-2026-48797/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-48797/
ATT&CKT1190 T1133
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • ML engineer intentionally running 'backprop ui --share' behind a VPN/reverse proxy that adds its own auth
  • Post-upgrade (>=1.2.0) hosts where --auth now works but the command line still contains the flag
  • CI/CD or lab automation launching the UI with --share for smoke tests
  • Operator using --share only on a firewalled internal network
  • Shell history / documentation examples echoed into a terminal without actually binding a port
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("ci-runner", "jenkins", "buildkite-agent")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the host's installed backpropagate version (pip show backpropagate / package inventory) — if >=1.2.0 the auth is enforced and risk is low; if 1.1.0-1.1.1 with --share, treat as an exposed unauthenticated training plane. 2) Contain: kill the Reflex process, block inbound to the bound port (default 7860) at the host firewall, and if the UI was internet-reachable revoke/rotate the operator's HuggingFace token (Hub-push abuse vector). 3) Hunt: pull inbound connections to port 7860 from non-loopback sources, review rx.upload writes for attacker-dropped datasets, and check HF push history for unexpected repo targets.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "backprop" and ProcessCommandLine has "ui"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "--share" or ProcessCommandLine has "--auth"
| extend PublicShare = ProcessCommandLine has "--share"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, PublicShare, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where (TargetProcessCommandLine has "backprop" or ActingProcessCommandLine has "backprop")
| where (TargetProcessCommandLine has "ui" or ActingProcessCommandLine has "ui")
| where (TargetProcessCommandLine has "--share" or TargetProcessCommandLine has "--auth" or ActingProcessCommandLine has "--share" or ActingProcessCommandLine has "--auth")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Surfaces endpoints with the Backpropagate package in the affected 1.1.0-1.1.1 range where the Reflex UI auth guard is inert. This is a posture/attack-surface hunt to prioritise upgrades to 1.2.0 before an exposed UI is reached, keyed on the CVE and the concrete package/version range from the advisory. Rationale: Keys on the exact CVE id (CVE-2026-48797) and the advisory's affected version range (pip:backpropagate >=1.1.0,<1.2.0), so it enumerates the concrete attack surface without guessing behaviour. Vuln-inventory telemetry only, no endpoint noise. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-48797/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/backpropagate/CVE-2026-48797/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-48797-cwe-358-improperly-implemented-secu-63bfdfdd
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities.Vulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Hosts already scheduled/queued for the 1.2.0 upgrade in the current patch window
  • Isolated air-gapped GPU boxes where the UI is never launched with --share
  • Dev sandboxes intentionally kept on 1.1.x for reproducibility that never bind the UI
  • TVM version parsing quirks reporting 1.1.x npm @mcptoolshop/backpropagate separately from the pip package
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reported version is truly 1.1.0 or 1.1.1 (not a mis-parsed 1.2.x) and whether the host has ever launched the Reflex UI. 2) Contain: schedule/push 'pip install --upgrade backpropagate' to >=1.2.0 (or @mcptoolshop/backpropagate@latest); if immediate upgrade is impossible, ensure the UI is only started with no flags (localhost bind) and reachable only via SSH port-forward. 3) Hunt: cross-reference these hosts against process-creation hits for 'backprop ui --share' and inbound connections to port 7860 to find any that were actually exposed.

Inbound access to exposed Backpropagate Reflex UI port 7860 from non-local source (CVE-2026-48797) AIExploitation Medium
Detects inbound network sessions to the Reflex training UI's default port 7860 originating from non-loopback/non-RFC1918 addresses. On an unpatched host launched with --share this is direct unauthenticated access to the training control plane (dataset read, training start, HuggingFace push), i.e. active exploitation rather than mere exposure. Rationale: Port 7860 is the Reflex/Gradio default the advisory itself references (ssh -L 7860:localhost:7860 workaround). Inbound from a public/non-RFC1918 source to that port on an unpatched host is the concrete exploitation shape for the unauthenticated UI, distinct from the process-launch and inventory pivots. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f65r-h4g3-3h9h • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-48797/
ATT&CKT1190 T1213
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate remote collaborator reaching a --share UI over a corporate egress IP that presents as public
  • Other apps that also default to port 7860 (Gradio demos, unrelated Reflex apps) on the same host
  • Vulnerability scanners / attack-surface tools probing 7860 across the estate
  • NAT/proxy hairpin making an internal client appear as a public source IP
  • Load-balancer or uptime-monitor health checks hitting the port
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("python", "python3", "reflex", "backprop", "node")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the listening process on 7860 is the Backpropagate/Reflex UI (InitiatingProcessFileName python/reflex) and the host version is 1.1.0-1.1.1; identify the remote source IP and whether it is expected. 2) Contain: block inbound 7860 at host/perimeter firewall, terminate the Reflex process, and rotate the operator's HuggingFace token if the session was reachable. 3) Hunt: review rx.upload file writes and dataset-preview reads during the session window, enumerate any HuggingFace Hub push targets, and check for repeated/high-volume uploads indicating disk-fill DoS.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where LocalPort == 7860
| where ActionType in ("InboundConnectionAccepted", "ListeningConnectionCreated")
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, LocalIP, LocalPort, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where DstPortNumber == 7860
| where NetworkDirection == "Inbound"
| where isnotempty(SrcIpAddr) and not(ipv4_is_private(SrcIpAddr)) and SrcIpAddr != "127.0.0.1"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, SrcIpAddr, NetworkApplicationProtocol, DvcAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "4a8860240e4231c3a74c81949be655a28e096a7d72f38fbe84e5b37636b98417", "ae70dd4f6bc0d1c8c2848e4e6b51934626c4818dcb5af99d080ddbd7dc337185", "2553649f2322049666871cea80a5d0d6adc700ca", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Incus daemon (incusd) writes file outside image store to cron/OS path (CVE-2026-48769) AIInstallation High
Hunts the arbitrary-file-write primitive of CVE-2026-48769: the incusd daemon creating or modifying a file under a root-executable location (cron dirs, sudoers.d, systemd units, ld.so.preload, root SSH, profile.d) as a result of a path-traversal Incus-Image-Hash header. incusd has no legitimate reason to write into these OS directories, so a hit is a near-certain exploitation of the direct-URL image import. Rationale: Uses the exact daemon binary (incusd), the exact image-store root the traversal escapes (/var/lib/incus/images/), and the root-executable sink directories (/etc/cron.d etc.) named in the PoC (../../../../etc/cron.d/incus-direct-image-url-rce). incusd writing outside its own data dir into an OS boot/cron path is effectively impossible in normal operation, giving a very low FP rate. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/lxc/incus/security/advisories/GHSA-f6m5-xw2g-xc4x • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/ghsa-f6m5-xw2g-xc4x • https://advisory.eventussecurity.com/advisory/incus-vulnerabilities-break-host-instance-boundary/
ATT&CKT1053.003 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A local admin running incusd with a non-default storage path and image store relocated outside /var/lib/incus (custom Incus config)
  • Incus instance provisioning hooks / cloud-init integration that legitimately drop a profile.d or systemd unit during container start
  • Configuration-management tooling (Ansible/Puppet) invoked under the incusd process tree writing OS files during host bootstrap
  • Package upgrades of Incus itself installing systemd units — attributed to dpkg/rpm, not incusd, but may co-occur
  • EDR mis-attributing a child helper process file write to the incusd parent
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath startswith "/var/lib/incus" or FolderPath startswith "/etc/incus")
Response runbook
1) Triage: Pull the offending file (FolderPath/FileName) and read its contents — CVE-2026-48769 writes a cron line like '* * * * * root /bin/sh -c <cmd>'. Confirm incusd version (< 6.23.0 = vulnerable) and check recent incus image import with source.type=url. 2) Contain: Delete the planted file, kill any cron/systemd job it spawned, isolate the host, and block/rotate any credentials the payload could reach; snapshot for forensics first. 3) Hunt: Enumerate all incusd file writes outside /var/lib/incus in the last 30d, review incusd outbound HTTP to unknown image servers, and check other hosts running vulnerable Incus for the same planted filenames.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "incusd"
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has_any ("/etc/cron.d","/etc/cron.daily","/etc/cron.hourly","/etc/cron.weekly","/etc/cron.monthly","/etc/crontab","/var/spool/cron","/etc/sudoers.d","/etc/ld.so.preload","/etc/profile.d","/root/.ssh","/etc/systemd/system")
| where FolderPath !startswith "/var/lib/incus/images"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ActingProcessName has "incusd"
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileCreate","FileRename")
| where TargetFilePath has_any ("/etc/cron.d","/etc/cron.daily","/etc/cron.hourly","/etc/crontab","/var/spool/cron","/etc/sudoers.d","/etc/ld.so.preload","/etc/profile.d","/root/.ssh","/etc/systemd/system")
| where TargetFilePath !startswith "/var/lib/incus/images"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, Hash
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Incus Daemon File Write Outside Image Store to Sensitive OS Path (CVE-2026-48769)
id: 6f1c9a4e-8d2b-4d7a-9c3e-2a1b5e0f9d44
description: Detects the incusd daemon creating or modifying a file under a root-executable OS location, indicative of the CVE-2026-48769 path-traversal Incus-Image-Hash arbitrary file write.
references:
  - https://github.com/lxc/incus/security/advisories/GHSA-f6m5-xw2g-xc4x
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f6m5-xw2g-xc4x
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/07

  product: linux
detection:
  selection_proc:
    Image|endswith: '/incusd'
  selection_path:
    TargetFilename|contains:
      - '/etc/cron.d/'
      - '/etc/cron.daily/'

      - '/etc/crontab'

      - '/etc/profile.d/'
      - '/root/.ssh/'
      - '/etc/systemd/system/'
  filter_imgdir:
    TargetFilename|startswith: '/var/lib/incus/images/'

Incus daemon outbound image fetch to public host followed by write outside data dir (CVE-2026-48769) AIDelivery Medium
Correlates the delivery and exploit halves of CVE-2026-48769: incusd making an outbound connection to a public IP (the attacker-controlled source.type=url image server) and, on the same host within minutes, writing a file anywhere outside /var/lib/incus. Gating the file-write on a preceding external fetch catches traversal to sinks the sensitive-dir list misses while suppressing normal local activity. Rationale: Ties the two article-specific behaviours: incusd reaching an external image server (source.type=url HEAD/GET) and then writing outside its own /var/lib/incus tree within a short window — the observable signature of the traversal write. Excluding incus's own /var/lib, /var/log and /etc paths keeps only escapes. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/lxc/incus/security/advisories/GHSA-f6m5-xw2g-xc4x • https://advisory.eventussecurity.com/advisory/incus-vulnerabilities-break-host-instance-boundary/
ATT&CKT1105 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate incus image import from a public image server (images.linuxcontainers.org) that coincides with unrelated incusd file activity on the host
  • incusd fetching a public URL image while normal container disk/backup writes land in a non-standard storage pool outside /var/lib/incus
  • Custom storage pool mounted outside /var/lib/incus causing benign incusd writes to appear as 'escapes'
  • Proxy/NAT egress making an internal mirror appear as a public RemoteIP
  • Short-window coincidence of an image pull and a scheduled Incus maintenance write
Suppression filter
| where RemoteUrl !endswith "images.linuxcontainers.org" and RemoteUrl !endswith "linuxcontainers.org"
Response runbook
1) Triage: Inspect the RemoteIP/RemoteUrl incusd contacted — is it a known image mirror or an unknown/attacker host? Read the written file for a cron/systemd/sudoers payload and confirm incusd < 6.23.0. 2) Contain: Remove the planted file, terminate spawned jobs, isolate the host, and block the remote image-server IP/domain at egress. 3) Hunt: Search all hosts for incusd connections to that RemoteIP and for any incusd writes outside /var/lib/incus; audit who issued the 'incus image import' with source.type=url and from which client/API token.
let Window = 10m;
let Fetches = DeviceNetworkEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(14d)
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "incusd"
    | where RemoteIPType == "Public"
    | project FetchTime = Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "incusd"
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where FolderPath !startswith "/var/lib/incus" and FolderPath !startswith "/var/log/incus" and FolderPath !startswith "/etc/incus"
| join kind=inner Fetches on DeviceName
| where Timestamp between (FetchTime .. FetchTime + Window)
| project FetchTime, WriteTime = Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by WriteTime desc

| where FolderPath !startswith "/var/lib/incus" and FolderPath !startswith "/var/log/incus" and FolderPath !startswith "/etc/incus"
| join kind=inner Fetches on DeviceName
| where TimeGenerated between (FetchTime .. FetchTime + Window)
| project FetchTime, WriteTime = TimeGenerated, DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by WriteTime desc
#event_simpleName=FileWritten ContextImageFileName=/incusd$/i
| NOT (TargetFileName=/\/var\/lib\/incus\//i)
| NOT (TargetFileName=/\/var\/log\/incus\//i)
| NOT (TargetFileName=/\/etc\/incus\//i)
| join({#event_simpleName=NetworkConnectIP4 ContextImageFileName=/incusd$/i | NOT cidr("10.0.0.0/8", field=RemoteAddressIP4) | NOT cidr("192.168.0.0/16", field=RemoteAddressIP4) | NOT cidr("172.16.0.0/12", field=RemoteAddressIP4)}, field=aid, include=[RemoteAddressIP4, RemotePort])
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName, RemoteAddressIP4], function=count())
ENDPOINT

Threat Brief: Mitigating Large-Scale Credential Attacks

Unit 42 (Palo Alto)
2026-06-26 11 use cases 2 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

We provide guidance for preparing for and mitigating large-scale credential attacks, focusing on recent campaigns targeting security vendors' devices. The post Threat Brief: Mitigating Large-Scale Credential Attacks appeared first on Unit 42 .

CVEsCVE-2024-55591 CVE-2025-59718 CVE-2025-59719

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender · Microsoft Teams external-tenant chat from unverified IT-helpdesk impersonator
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] FortiBleed password-spray success burst against internet-facing Fortinet/edge auth · [LLM] FortiGate jsconsole / loopback admin login (CVE-2024-55591 auth-bypass exploitation) · [LLM] FortiBleed MSSQL login-failure burst then success (internet-exposed SQL spraying) · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] FortiGate rogue super_admin / SSL-VPN account creation following CVE-2024-55591 exploit · RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
FortiBleed password-spray success burst against internet-facing Fortinet/edge auth AIExploitation Medium
Hunts a single source IP producing a high volume of failed authentications against a Fortinet appliance (SSL-VPN / admin login) followed by a successful login in the same window — the exact 'successful logins shortly after large-volume password failure events' pattern Unit 42 flags for the FortiBleed credential-spraying campaign. Rationale: Implements Unit 42's explicit FortiBleed guidance — audit remote-access logs for successful logins shortly after large-volume failure events — scoped to Fortinet auth via the CIM app values / DeviceVendor='Fortinet'. Threshold + same-window success join is what separates spray-then-compromise from routine VPN typos. Cross-checked with SOCRadar/SecurityWeek FortiBleed reporting and Arctic Wolf's CVE-2024-55591 analysis. Cross-checked against: • https://socradar.io/blog/what-is-fortibleed/ • https://www.securityweek.com/fortibleed-86000-fortinet-device-credentials-compromised/ • https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/cve-2024-55591/
ATT&CKT1110.003 T1110.004 T1133
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate remote worker repeatedly fat-fingering an SSL-VPN password then succeeding
  • Misconfigured VPN client or saved-credential loop retrying stale creds before a successful re-auth
  • Authenticated vulnerability scanner (Qualys/Tenable) credential checks against the appliance
  • Shared NAT egress IP where many staff log in (inflated failure+success counts from one SourceIP)
  • Password-rotation day causing widespread first-attempt failures followed by success
Suppression filter
| where SourceIP !in ("<populate watchlist: known scanner + corp NAT egress IPs via a Sentinel watchlist join, not inline>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the CompromisedUser + SourceIP; geolocate SourceIP and check whether the success used MFA and from a known ASN/country. Inspect the failed-user set — many distinct users from one IP = spray, not a typo. 2) Contain: if SourceIP is foreign/unrecognized, force-disable the compromised admin/VPN account, kill active SSL-VPN tunnels, block the SourceIP at the edge, and rotate the credential. 3) Hunt: from that SourceIP/account pivot to post-auth config changes (new admin accounts, sslvpn user groups), jsconsole admin logins, and any config download — feed into the FortiBleed jsconsole and rogue-admin UCs.
let lookback = 7d;
let window = 1h;
let Failed = CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Activity has "login" and (EventOutcome in~ ("failure","failed") or Message has_any ("login failed","Login failed","authentication failed","ssl-login-fail"))
| summarize Failures = count(), TargetUsers = dcount(DestinationHostName) by SourceIP, bin(TimeGenerated, window);
let Success = CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Activity has "login" and (EventOutcome in~ ("success","succeeded") or Message has_any ("login successful","SSL tunnel established"))
| summarize Successes = count(), CompromisedUser = make_set(DestinationHostName, 20) by SourceIP, bin(TimeGenerated, window);
Failed
| where Failures >= 30   // 30 = empirical spray burst floor per source per hour
| join kind=inner Success on SourceIP, TimeGenerated
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, Failures, TargetUsers, Successes, CompromisedUser
| order by Failures desc
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app IN ("fortios","fortigate","fortiproxy","ftnt-fortigate","sslvpn-plain","forticlient_ems") by Authentication.src, Authentication.dest, Authentication.user, Authentication.action, _time span=1h
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
| stats sum(eval(if(action="failure",count,0))) as failures, sum(eval(if(action="success",count,0))) as successes, dc(user) as targeted_users, values(eval(if(action="success",user,null()))) as compromised_user, values(dest) as dest by src, _time
| where failures >= 30 and successes >= 1
| sort - failures
| tstats summariesonly=false count from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app IN ("fortios","fortigate","fortiproxy","ftnt-fortigate","sslvpn-plain","forticlient_ems") by Authentication.src, Authentication.dest, Authentication.user, Authentication.action, _time span=1h
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
| stats sum(eval(if(action="failure",count,0))) as failures, sum(eval(if(action="success",count,0))) as successes, dc(user) as targeted_users, values(eval(if(action="success",user,null()))) as compromised_user, values(dest) as dest by src, _time
| where failures >= 30 and successes >= 1
| sort - failures
FortiGate jsconsole / loopback admin login (CVE-2024-55591 auth-bypass exploitation) AIExploitation High
Hunts the high-fidelity exploitation signature of CVE-2024-55591: administrative logins attributed to the 'jsconsole' interface and/or successful super-admin logins sourced from the device loopback/local IP — the artifact the Node.js websocket auth-bypass leaves when an unauthenticated attacker forges super_admin access on FortiOS/FortiProxy. Rationale: Uses the two corroborated CVE-2024-55591 exploitation artifacts — `ui=jsconsole` in the admin-login record (matched in CommonSecurityLog Message) and super-admin logins from the device loopback/local IP (CIM Authentication.src=127.0.0.1) — reported by Arctic Wolf, watchTowr ('I Am The Super_Admin Now') and Trustwave. Both are near-zero in benign FortiGate operation. Cross-checked against: • https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/cve-2024-55591/ • https://labs.watchtowr.com/get-fortirekt-i-am-the-super_admin-now-fortios-authentication-bypass-cve-2024-55591/ • https://trustwave.com/en-us/resources/blogs/spiderlabs-blog/cve-2024-55591-fortinet-fortios-fortiproxy-zero-day
ATT&CKT1190 T1078.001 T1556
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin legitimately running CLI commands through the FortiGate GUI 'CLI console' widget (also tagged jsconsole) — distinguish by source being a real admin IP, not loopback
  • Automation/orchestration tooling (Ansible/FortiManager) driving CLI via the local console
  • Internal penetration test replicating CVE-2024-55591
  • Health-check scripts hitting the management interface from a jump host
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the jsconsole/loopback login corresponds to a real admin session — legitimate jsconsole use originates from an authenticated GUI session with a genuine admin SourceIP; exploitation shows loopback/local src and no preceding interactive auth. Check FortiOS version against the CVE-2024-55591 fixed builds. 2) Contain: isolate the device's management interface from the internet, disable/rotate all admin accounts, and capture the running + startup config for IR. 3) Hunt: enumerate admin accounts and sslvpn user groups created/modified after the first jsconsole login, look for `--userfrom`-style audit-record tampering, and any config download/exfil to chain with the rogue-admin and FortiBleed config-extraction UCs.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Message has "jsconsole"
| where Message has_any ("login","logged in","logged-in","admin")
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceProduct, DeviceExternalID, SourceIP, AdminUser = SourceUserName, DestinationHostName, Activity, EventOutcome, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`

FortiGate rogue super_admin / SSL-VPN account creation following CVE-2024-55591 exploit AIInstallation High
Hunts post-exploitation persistence for the FortiBleed/CVE-2024-55591 chain: creation of new FortiGate administrator accounts (often random usernames) or new SSL-VPN local users / user groups via config changes. This is how attackers convert a one-shot auth bypass into durable, high-privilege access for offline-cracked credential re-use. Rationale: Targets the documented CVE-2024-55591 post-exploit actions — new admin accounts with random names, new SSL-VPN local users and groups — via Fortinet config-change logs (cfgpath system.admin / user.group) and the CIM Change datamodel (action=created, object_category=user). Account creation on an edge appliance is rare and tightly change-controlled, so FP is low. Corroborated by Arctic Wolf and Cyble. Cross-checked against: • https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/cve-2024-55591/ • https://cyble.com/blog/cve-2024-55591-the-fortinet-flaw-putting-critical-systems-at-risk/
ATT&CKT1136 T1098 T1133
Data sourcesChange.All_Changes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Planned admin onboarding by the network team during a change window
  • FortiManager/automation pushing a config template that provisions admin or VPN users
  • Legitimate SSL-VPN user-group expansion during an access-request rollout
  • MSP/managed-SOC provisioning a monitoring admin account
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the ActorAdmin and SourceIP that created the new object; correlate against an authorized change ticket and verify the actor session was not a jsconsole/loopback exploit login. Flag random-looking usernames. 2) Contain: delete or disable the unauthorized admin/VPN account and group, terminate its sessions, and rotate credentials for every legitimate admin on the device. 3) Hunt: review whether the new account granted VPN access, established tunnels into internal networks, or downloaded the device config; pivot on its SourceIP across other edge devices to scope the FortiBleed campaign footprint.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Message has_any ("system.admin","user.local","user.group")
| where Message has_any ("super_admin","prof_admin","accprofile","sslvpn")
| where Message has_any ("Object attribute configured","Added","cfgattr","New","create")
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceProduct, SourceIP, ActorAdmin = SourceUserName, NewObject = DestinationHostName, Activity, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc

FortiBleed MSSQL login-failure burst then success (internet-exposed SQL spraying) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the FortiBleed campaign's parallel targeting of internet-exposed MSSQL: a single client IP generating a burst of SQL Server login failures (error 18456) across one or many logins within a short window — the spray signature Unit 42 observed against MSSQL devices alongside Fortinet and Sophos. Rationale: Covers the article's explicit observation that the FortiBleed actors also targeted MSSQL devices. Pivots on SQL Server error 18456 (login failed) parsed for CLIENT IP and login name in Sentinel's Event table, with a Defender DeviceLogonEvents fallback for Windows-auth brute force against the SQL host and a CIM Authentication path for Splunk. Threshold-based, so framed as a burst detector. Cross-checked against: • https://socradar.io/blog/what-is-fortibleed/ • https://www.securityweek.com/fortibleed-86000-fortinet-device-credentials-compromised/
ATT&CKT1110.003 T1078
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Application/service account with a stale connection string hammering the DB after a password change
  • Linked-server or ETL job misconfigured with wrong credentials retrying in a tight loop
  • Monitoring agent (SCOM/SolarWinds) probing SQL with expired creds
  • Authenticated vulnerability scanner credential audit against the SQL instance
  • Developer load-test pointing at the wrong SQL host
Suppression filter
| where AccountName !in~ ("sqlmonitor","svc-etl","svc-backup")   // tune to your known SQL service accounts
Response runbook
1) Triage: resolve ClientIP — internal app server vs external/unknown. Inspect the targeted login set: many distinct logins from one IP = spray; a single service account = likely stale credential. Check for any 18454/successful login from the same ClientIP right after the burst. 2) Contain: if external, block the ClientIP at the firewall, confirm the SQL instance is not directly internet-exposed, and disable SQL-auth logins that were sprayed; enforce that MSSQL sits behind the VPN/jump box. 3) Hunt: if a success followed, review that login's subsequent activity (xp_cmdshell, new SQL logins, linked-server abuse) and pivot the ClientIP across Fortinet edge logs to tie it to the broader FortiBleed spraying infrastructure.
DeviceLogonEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "LogonFailed"
| where LogonType in ("Network","Batch")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| summarize Failures = count(), TargetedAccounts = dcount(AccountName), Accounts = make_set(AccountName, 30), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp) by RemoteIP, DeviceName, bin(Timestamp, 30m)
| where Failures >= 30   // 30 failed network logons / 30 min from one source = spray
| order by Failures desc
let lookback = 7d;
let window = 30m;
Event
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where Source == "MSSQLSERVER"
| where EventID == 18456   // SQL Server: Login failed
| extend LoginUser = extract(@"Login failed for user '([^']+)'", 1, RenderedDescription)
| extend ClientIP = extract(@"\[CLIENT: ([0-9A-Fa-f\.:]+)\]", 1, RenderedDescription)
| where isnotempty(ClientIP)
| summarize Failures = count(), TargetedLogins = dcount(LoginUser), Logins = make_set(LoginUser, 30) by ClientIP, Computer, bin(TimeGenerated, window)
| where Failures >= 30
| order by Failures desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`

| where failures >= 30
